Figure 39.
E
leaves one external scar

Having established the claims, we now prove the Lemma: Suppose a ball
B_{G}\subset\mbox{${\rm int}$}(H)
presents itself, as described before Claim 1. Claim 1 says that
E
leaves scars on
G
, claims 2 and 3 show that
E
leaves exactly one scar on
G
, and Claim 4 says that it cannot be an internal scar. Thus
E
must leave one external scar on
G
, in which case Claim 5 shows that one of the three outcomes of Lemma
12.11
occurs.

On the other hand, if no
B_{G}
satisfies the criteria described before Claim 1 then Claims 1, 2, 3 and 4 show that, unless
\mbox{$\partial$}H
contains a single external scar of
E
,
H
is coherent. Claim 5 shows that when
\mbox{$\partial$}H
does contain a single external scar of
E
, one of the three outcomes of Lemma
12.11
occurs.

We now examine when neither of the first two outcomes occurs, to verify the final three claims of Lemma
12.11
. We have already concluded that
E
is not parallel to any disk in
\mathcal{D}_{x}
. The proof so far shows also that the target manifold must have a single external scar, and then Claim 5 shows that in this case
\mbox{$\partial$}H
contains exactly one scar of
E
, an internal scar when the target manifold is
B_{G}
and an external scar when the target manifold is
H
.
All that remains is to show that
\mbox{$\partial$}E
is separating in
F-\mbox{$\partial$}\mathcal{D}_{x}
:

The definition of
F_{x}
implies that the complement of the scars of
\mathcal{D}_{x}
in
F_{x}
is a collection of components of
F-\mbox{$\partial$}\mathcal{D}_{x}
. Not removing the scars of the disks in
\mathcal{D}_{x}
, that is, adding disks back, does not affect the number of components in the surface, so to show that
\mbox{$\partial$}E
is separating in a component of
F-\mbox{$\partial$}D_{x}
it suffices to show that
\mbox{$\partial$}E
is separating in a component of
F_{x}
. But that follows immediately from the fact that
E
leaves only one scar on
\mbox{$\partial$}H
:
\mbox{$\partial$}H
is a component of
F_{x}-\mbox{$\partial$}E
that is distinct from the component on which the other scar of
E
lies.
∎

Proposition 12.12
.

Suppose
\mathbb{C}
is a flagged chamber complex supporting
S^{3}=A\cup_{T}B
,
\mathbb{C}
does not certify and
\mathcal{D}_{x},\mathcal{D}_{y}
are disjoint disk sets in
\mathbb{C}
. If either
|\mathcal{D}_{x}|
or
|\mathcal{D}_{y}|=1
, then, after an appropriate choice of sibling if
\mathbb{C}_{x}
contains occupied handlebody chambers, either
\mathcal{D}_{xy}=\mathcal{D}_{x+y}
or
\mathcal{D}_{xy}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{D}_{x+y}
.

In more detail: Suppose
\mathcal{D}\cup E
is a disk set in
\mathbb{C}
, where
E
is a single disk. Let

•

\mathbb{C}_{DE}
be the flagged chamber complex given by the composition

\mathbb{C}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}}\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{D}}\xrightarrow{E}\mathbb{C}_{DE}

•

\mathbb{C}_{ED}
be the flagged chamber complex given by the composition

\mathbb{C}\xrightarrow{E}\mathbb{C}_{E}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}}\mathbb{C}_{ED}

and
\mathcal{D}
the preferred alignment described in Lemma
12.8

•

\mathbb{C}_{D+E}
be the flagged chamber complex given by the decomposition

\mathbb{C}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}\cup E}\mathbb{C}_{D+E}
.

(Note that here
D
in the subscripts refers not to a single disk but to the family
\mathcal{D}
of disks.)

Then either

•

\mathbb{C}_{DE}=\mathbb{C}_{D+E}=\mathbb{C}_{ED}
or

•

\mathbb{C}_{DE}=\mathbb{C}_{D+E}\dashleftarrow\mathbb{C}_{ED}
or

•

\mathbb{C}_{DE}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{C}_{D+E}=\mathbb{C}_{ED}
or

•

E
lies in an occupied handlebody in
\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{D}}
and its sibling
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{DE}
satisfies

\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{DE}=\mathbb{C}_{D+E}=\mathbb{C}_{ED}

If
\mathbb{C}_{D+E}\neq\mathbb{C}_{ED}
then either
\mbox{$\partial$}E
is non-separating in
F-\mbox{$\partial$}\mathcal{D}
or
\mbox{$\partial$}E
is non-separating in
F
and
E
is parallel to a disk in
\mathcal{D}
.

Proof.

The proof breaks naturally into two cases:

Case 1:

\mathbb{C}_{ED}\neq\mathbb{C}_{D+E}
.

By Lemma
12.3
there is a handlebody
H
in
S^{3}
whose boundary is in
F_{\mathcal{D}\cup E}
and which is not coherent in
\mathbb{C}_{ED}
and
\mathbb{C}_{D+E}
. Then applying Lemma
12.10
to
H
, either
\mbox{$\partial$}E
is non-separating in
F-\mbox{$\partial$}\mathcal{D}
or
\mbox{$\partial$}E
is non-separating in
F
and
E
is parallel to a disk in
\mathcal{D}
. Then by Lemma
12.11
every handlebody in
S^{3}
whose boundary is in
F_{\mathcal{D}\cup E}
is coherent for
\mathbb{C}_{DE}
and
\mathbb{C}_{D+E}
. So, by Lemma
12.3
,
\mathbb{C}_{DE}=\mathbb{C}_{D+E}
. Thus what is left to show in this case is that
\mathbb{C}_{ED}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{C}_{D+E}
.

There cannot be two disjoint handlebodies in
S^{3}
whose boundaries are in
F_{\mathcal{D}\cup E}
and which are not coherent for
\mathbb{C}_{ED}
and
\mathbb{C}_{D+E}
since Lemma
12.10
says that the disk
E
lies in the interior of each such handlebody. Then there is a maximal such handlebody - that is, one that contains any other - so without loss take
H
to be the maximal one. By Lemma
12.10

H
is contained in a chamber of
\mathbb{C}_{ED}
and is obtained from a chamber of
\mathbb{C}_{D+E}
by deflation of a chamber or of a bullseye in
\mathbb{C}_{D+E}
. All chambers of
\mathbb{C}_{D+E}
outside of
H
are the same in
\mathbb{C}_{D+E}
and
\mathbb{C}_{ED}
by the same proof as that of Lemma
12.3
. Thus
\mathbb{C}_{ED}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{C}_{D+E}
as required.

Case 2:

\mathbb{C}_{DE}\neq\mathbb{C}_{D+E}

The proof is analogous to that of Case 1, but a bit more complicated. By Lemma
12.3
there is a handlebody
H
in
S^{3}
whose boundary is in
F_{\mathcal{D}\cup E}
and which is not coherent in
\mathbb{C}_{DE}
and
\mathbb{C}_{D+E}
. Then according to Lemma
12.11

E
is not parallel to a disk in
\mathcal{D}
and
\mbox{$\partial$}E
is separating in
F-\mbox{$\partial$}\mathcal{D}
. Then by Lemma
12.10
every handlebody in
S^{3}
whose boundary is in
F_{\mathcal{D}\cup E}
is coherent for
\mathbb{C}_{ED}
and
\mathbb{C}_{D+E}
so by Lemma
12.3

\mathbb{C}_{ED}=\mathbb{C}_{D+E}
. Thus what is left to show in this case is that
\mathbb{C}_{DE}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{C}_{D+E}
.

According to Lemma
12.11

E
leaves exactly one scar on
\mbox{$\partial$}H
. If it is an internal scar, then per Claim 5 in the proof of Lemma
12.11
, if
E
lies in the interior of a chamber
C_{x}
of
\mathbb{C}_{x}
dividing
C_{x}
into a ball in
\mbox{${\rm int}$}(H)
and a chamber
R_{x}
adjacent to and outside of
H
, as in Figure
38
. Unless
R_{x}
is also a handlebody, the choice of preferred alignment of
E
has no effect on the flagging of chambers outside of
H
. In particular, there can be no handlebody chamber incoherent for
\mathbb{C}_{DE}
and
\mathbb{C}_{D+E}
that is disjoint from
H
. In this case, the argument concludes as for Case 1, using Lemma
12.11
instead of Lemma
12.10
. On the other hand, if
R_{x}
is a handlebody (and so, as noted in Lemma
12.11
Claim 5, Subcase 1a, the only chamber of
\mathbb{C}_{x+y}
that lies outside of
H
) then the choice of alignment of
E
in
C_{x}
does affect the flagging of
R_{x}
. The argument in this case is much like the argument in the case that
E
leaves an external scar on
H
, so we turn to that case, noting that the argument applies here as well.

If
E
leaves an external scar on
H
then it is possible that there is a disjoint handlebody
H^{\prime}
in
S^{3}
just like
H
:
\mbox{$\partial$}H^{\prime}
is in
F_{\mathcal{D}\cup E}
,
H^{\prime}
is not coherent for
\mathbb{C}_{DE}
and
\mathbb{C}_{D+E}
, and
E
also leaves an external scar on
\mbox{$\partial$}H^{\prime}
. Then, just as is true for
H
,
H^{\prime}
lies in a handlebody chamber in
\mathbb{C}_{DE}
. Thus there is a handlebody chamber
C_{x}\in\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{D}}
containing
E
, with
E
dividing
C_{x}
into
H
and
H^{\prime}
.

We now reprise part of the argument of Claim 4a in Lemma
12.10
:
C_{x}
must be an occupied handlebody, for if
C_{x}
is empty then
H
and
H^{\prime}
would both be empty in
\mathbb{C}_{D+E}
and
\mathbb{C}_{DE}
, contradicting the assumption that
H
is not coherent. Then, per Definition
7.2
,
C_{x}
is the parent of two siblings in
\mathbb{C}_{DE}
: one in which
H
is occupied and
H^{\prime}
is empty, the other in which
H^{\prime}
is occupied and
H
is empty.

Since
C_{x}
is occupied,
F\cap\mbox{${\rm int}$}(C_{x})
has non-disk components by Corollary
7.3
. There are three cases to consider, depending on where these non-disk components lie:

•

If some non-disk components lie in
H
and some in
H^{\prime}
, then
H
and
H^{\prime}
are both occupied in
\mathbb{C}_{D+E}
. Since
H
is not coherent, it must then be empty in
\mathbb{C}_{DE}
. In this case the sibling for
C_{DE}
is the one in which
H
is empty and
H^{\prime}
is occupied, that is the one that results from an alignment for
E
which deflates
H
into
H^{\prime}
, leaving
H^{\prime}
occupied in
\mathbb{C}_{DE}
as well. Thus
\mathbb{C}_{DE}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{C}_{D+E}
, an allowed outcome.

•

If each component of
F\cap\mbox{${\rm int}$}(H)
is a disk, then
H
is empty in
\mathbb{C}_{D+E}
. Since
H
is not coherent it is then occupied in
\mathbb{C}_{DE}
. But in this case the sibling
C^{\prime}_{DE}
of
C_{DE}
is one in which
H
is empty and
H^{\prime}
is occupied. Then in this sibling
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{DE}=\mathbb{C}_{D+E}
, an allowed outcome.

•

If each component of
F\cap\mbox{${\rm int}$}(H^{\prime})
is a disk then
H^{\prime}
is empty and
H
is occupied in
\mathbb{C}_{D+E}
. Since
H
is not coherent, it is empty in
\mathbb{C}_{DE}
. In this case the sibling
C^{\prime}_{DE}
of
C_{DE}
has
H
occupied and
H^{\prime}
empty, just as is true for
\mathbb{C}_{D+E}
. Thus
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{DE}=\mathbb{C}_{D+E}
, an allowed outcome.

∎

Corollary 12.13
.

Suppose
\mathbb{C}
is a flagged chamber complex supporting
S^{3}=A\cup_{T}B
,
\mathbb{C}
does not certify and
\mathcal{D}\cup E
is a disk set in
\mathbb{C}
, where
E
is a single disk. In the notation of Proposition
12.12
, one of these is true (for some choice of sibling, if siblings exist):

•

\mathbb{C}_{DE}=\mathbb{C}_{ED}
,

•

\mathbb{C}_{DE}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{C}_{ED}
or

•

\mathbb{C}_{DE}\dashleftarrow\mathbb{C}_{ED}

We denote the three possibilities above by
\mathbb{C}_{DE}\dashleftrightarrow\mathbb{C}_{ED}
. Then one way of expressing the conclusion of Corollary
12.13
is to say that this diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\mathbb{C}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{D}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{E}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}_{DE}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}_{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{D}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}_{ED}}

13.
Guiding disk sets and disk addition

Recall from Section
11
the notion of guiding disks (Definition
11.8
) for flagged chamber decompositions. The example given there involved decomposition of two chamber complexes that are related by bullseye deflation. Here is another example: Suppose
\mathcal{D}\cup E
is a disk set in a flagged chamber complex
\mathbb{C}
. Let
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}
be the result of the flagged chamber complex decomposition
\mathbb{C}\xrightarrow{E}\mathbb{C}^{\prime}
and let
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
be the set of disks in
\mathcal{D}
whose boundaries lie on
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}
. The decomposing set
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
is contained in
\mathcal{D}
, but may not be all of
\mathcal{D}
, because it does include those disks in
\mathcal{D}
that lie on goneballs of the decomposition
\mathbb{C}\xrightarrow{E}\mathbb{C}^{\prime}
. Nevertheless
\mathcal{D}
remains as a guiding set of disks for the decomposition
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}
.

We have already encountered another example of guiding disk sets, in the context of guiding spheres in Section
9
. Suppose
\mathbb{C}
is a flagged chamber complex in
S^{3}
with defining surface
F=F(\mathbb{C})
, and
S\subset S^{3}
is an embedded sphere transverse to
F
. Consider a sequence
\vec{\mathbb{C}}
of flagged chamber complex decompositions guided by
S
, as given in Definition
9.4
and described after the proof of Lemma
9.3
: The circles
F\cap S
are partitioned into sets
\mathfrak{c}_{i},0\leq i\leq n
and to each
\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}_{i}
a collection of disjoint disks
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{i}\subset S
is assigned. The associated sequence
\vec{\mathbb{C}}
of flagged chamber complex decompositions guided by
S
has, as each disk set
\mathcal{D}_{i}
those disks in
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
that remain incident to
F(\mathbb{C}_{i})
after all goneballs in earlier decompositions have been removed. Thus each set of disks
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
is a guiding set of disks for the decomposition
\mathbb{C}_{i}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}_{i}}\mathbb{C}_{i+1}
not the disk set itself. Consistent with the notation of Definition
11.8
we could then write the sequence in Definition
9.4
as

\mathbb{C}=\mathbb{C}_{0}\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}\mathbb{C}_{1}\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{1}}\mathbb{C}_{2}\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{2}}...\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{k-1}}\mathbb{C}_{k}

Joining this example with the previous one, suppose
E
is a properly embedded disk in
\mathbb{C}
that is disjoint from the guiding sphere
S
and, as above, let
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}
be the result of the flagged chamber complex decomposition
\mathbb{C}\xrightarrow{E}\mathbb{C}^{\prime}
. Then it is natural to construct a sequence
\vec{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}
of flagged chamber complex decompositions

\vec{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}:\quad\mathbb{C}^{\prime}=\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{0}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{0}}\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{1}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{1}}\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{2}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{2}}...\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{n-1}}\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{n}

by setting each
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{i}
to be the collection of disks in
\mathcal{D}_{i}
(and so in
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
) whose boundaries lie on
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{i}
. Then, just as for
\vec{\mathbb{C}}
, each decomposition
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{i}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{i}}\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{i+1}
has guiding disk set
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
. So the sequence could also be written

\vec{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}:\quad\mathbb{C}^{\prime}=\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{0}\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{1}\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{1}}\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{2}\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{2}}...\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{n-1}}\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{n}.

One way to describe the relation between the sequences above is that the sequence
\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime}
is what the sequence
\vec{\mathbb{C}}
would be if we had inserted decomposition with the disk
E
before beginning the sequence. Generalizing, we could decompose by
E
at a later stage of the decomposition sequence. That is, for each
0\leq k\leq n
, define a flagged chamber complex decomposition sequence

\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{E}^{k}:\mathbb{C}_{0}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}_{0}}\mathbb{C}_{1}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}_{1}}...\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}_{k-1}}\mathbb{C}_{k}\xrightarrow{E}\mathbb{C}^{k}_{k}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}^{k}_{k}}\mathbb{C}^{k}_{k+1}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}^{k}_{k+1}}...\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}^{k}_{n-1}}\mathbb{C}^{k}_{n}

where the disks
\mathcal{D}^{k}_{i}\subset\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{i},k\leq i\leq n
are those disks whose boundaries lie on
F(\mathbb{C}^{k}_{i})
. In particular,
\vec{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}
of Sequence
13
.1 is
\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{E}^{0}
with the first decomposition
\mathbb{C}\xrightarrow{E}\mathbb{C}^{\prime}
dropped. As usual, a simplified way of writing each sequence is

\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{E}^{k}:\mathbb{C}_{0}\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}\mathbb{C}_{1}\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{1}}...\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{k-1}}\mathbb{C}_{k}\xrightarrow{E}\mathbb{C}^{k}_{k}\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{k}}\mathbb{C}^{k}_{k+1}\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{k+1}}...\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{n-1}}\mathbb{C}^{k}_{n}

Lemma 13.1
.

Suppose
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}
are flagged chamber complexes and their defining surfaces have the property that
F(\mathbb{C}^{\prime})\subset F(\mathbb{C})
. Suppose
\mathbb{C}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}}\mathbb{C}_{\mathcal{D}}
and
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}
have a guiding disk set
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
in common. Then
\mathcal{D}
is a guiding disk set for
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Each
D^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
is a disk in
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
whose boundary lies on
F(\mathbb{C}^{\prime})
. Since
F(\mathbb{C}^{\prime})\subset F(\mathbb{C})
,
\mbox{$\partial$}D^{\prime}
also lies on
F(\mathbb{C})
, so
D^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}
. Thus
D^{\prime}
is a disk in
\mathcal{D}
whose boundary lies in
F(\mathbb{C}^{\prime})
. Conversely, suppose
D\in\mathcal{D}
has its boundary on
F(\mathbb{C}^{\prime})
. Since
D\in\mathcal{D}
,
D\in\overline{\mathcal{D}}
and, since
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
is a guiding disk set for
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
and
\mbox{$\partial$}D\subset F(\mathbb{C}^{\prime})
,
D\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
. Thus
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
consists exactly of those disks in
\mathcal{D}
whose boundaries lie on
F(\mathbb{C}^{\prime})
, as required.
∎

Proposition 13.2
.

Let
\mathbb{C}
be a flagged chamber complex that supports
S^{3}=A\cup_{T}B
and

\vec{\mathbb{C}}:\mathbb{C}=\mathbb{C}_{0}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}_{0}}\mathbb{C}_{1}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}_{1}}...\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}_{n-1}}\mathbb{C}_{n}

be a flagged chamber complex decomposition sequence. Suppose

•

there is a guiding disk set
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
for each decomposition
\mathbb{C}_{i}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}_{i}}\mathbb{C}_{i+1}
.

•

E
is a proper disk in a chamber of
\mathbb{C}
, and
E
is disjoint from all disks in all
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{i}

•

\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime}
is the sequence defined in Formula
13
.1 above, so each
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
is a guiding disk set for
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{i}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{i}}\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{i+1}

•

for each
0\leq k\leq n
and decomposition sequence
\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{E}^{k}
defined in
13
.2 above each
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
is also a guiding disk set for the corresponding decomposition in
\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{E}^{k}
.

If
\mathbb{C}_{n}
,
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{n}
and all
\mathbb{C}_{n}^{k},0\leq k\leq n

certify, then the sequences
\vec{\mathbb{C}}
and
\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime}
cocertify.

Proof.

Case 1:

\mathbb{C}
certifies.

Then the sequence
\vec{\mathbb{C}}
and the sequence

\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{E}^{0}:\mathbb{C}\xrightarrow{E}\mathbb{C}^{\prime}=\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{0}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{0}}\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{1}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{1}}...\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{n-1}}\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{n}

cocertify because the chamber complex
\mathbb{C}
certifies for both. (The same argument shows that all sequences
\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{E}^{k}
cocertify.) Similarly the sequences
\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{E}^{0}
and
\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime}
cocertify because, by hypothesis,
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{n}
certifies and, as noted above, the latter sequence is contained in the former sequence (by dropping
\mathbb{C}\xrightarrow{E}\mathbb{C}^{\prime}
). Thus we have
\vec{\mathbb{C}}\sim\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{E}^{0}\sim\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime}
as required.

Case 2:

\mathbb{C}
does not certify, but
\mathbb{C}_{1}
does.

Since
\mathbb{C}
does not certify then per Corollary
12.13
, with some choice of siblings if there are siblings, the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{D}_{0}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{D}_{1}}
\textstyle{...}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{D}_{n-1}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}_{n}}
\textstyle{=\vec{\mathbb{C}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}^{1}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{D}^{1}_{1}}
\textstyle{...}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{D}^{1}_{n-1}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}^{1}_{n}}
\textstyle{=\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{E}^{1}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{0}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{1}}
\textstyle{...}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{n-1}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{n}}
\textstyle{=\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime}}

We henceforth ignore the issue of choosing siblings, since different choices of sibling give cocertifying sequences, via the certifying parent chamber. (See the proof of Proposition
9.5
.)

We would like to apply Corollary
11.12
to the sequences
\vec{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}
and
\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{E}^{1}
. The Corollary requires that if
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{1}
, say, deflates to
\mathbb{C}^{1}_{1}
(i. e.
\mathbb{C}^{1}_{1}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{1})
that the disks in
\mathcal{D}_{1}^{1}
are a subset of the disks in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{1}
, namely those that have their boundaries on the defining surface for
\mathbb{C}_{1}^{1}
. In our language,
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{1}
needs to be a guiding disk set for
\mathcal{D}^{1}_{1}
. Moreover, it is required that the same true for each
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{i}
and
\mathcal{D}^{1}_{i}
throughout the maximal deflationary sequence. But since, by hypothesis,
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
is a guiding set of disks for both
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{i}
and
\mathcal{D}^{1}_{i}
, this is true, via Lemma
13.1
.

So we can apply Corollary
11.12
: Since
\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{1}_{E}
certifies by hypothesis,
\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{1}_{E}
and
\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime}
cocertify. A symmetric argument leads to the same conclusion in the case that
\mathbb{C}^{1}_{1}
deflates to
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{1}
or, most simply, when
\mathbb{C}^{1}_{1}=\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{1}
. Since, in this case, we are assuming that
\mathbb{C}_{1}
certifies, it certifies for the sequences that contain it, namely
\vec{C}
, and
\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{E}^{1}
. So we have
\vec{\mathbb{C}}\sim\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{E}^{1}\sim\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime}
, as required.

We are left with the possibility that
\mathbb{C}_{1}
does not certify. The argument just concluded suggests an inductive approach, which we now pursue:

Inductive Step:
Suppose the sequence
\vec{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}
and, for
0\leq k\leq\ell
, all
\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{E}^{k}
cocertify. (Cases 1 and 2 show that this is true for
\ell=1
.) Then either
\vec{\mathbb{C}}\sim\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime}
and we are done, or the same statement is true with
\ell
replaced by
\ell+1
.

If
\mathbb{C}_{\ell}
certifies, then it certifies for sequences that contain it, including
\vec{\mathbb{C}}
, and
\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{E}^{\ell}
. Thus
\vec{\mathbb{C}}\sim\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{E}^{\ell}
. By inductive hypothesis,
\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{E}^{\ell}\sim\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime}
. Hence
\vec{\mathbb{C}}\sim\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime}
, as required.

If
\mathbb{C}_{\ell}
does not certify, Corollary
12.13
says the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\mathbb{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{D}_{0}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{D}_{1}}
\textstyle{...\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{D}_{\ell-1}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}_{\ell}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{D}_{\ell}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}_{\ell+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{D}_{\ell+1}}
\textstyle{...}
\textstyle{=\vec{\mathbb{C}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}^{\ell+1}_{\ell+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{D}^{\ell+1}_{\ell+1}}
\textstyle{...}
\textstyle{=\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{E}^{\ell+1}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}^{\ell}_{\ell}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{D}^{\ell}_{\ell}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}^{\ell}_{\ell+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{D}^{\ell}_{\ell+1}}
\textstyle{...}
\textstyle{=\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{E}^{\ell}}

Applying Corollary
11.12
much as in Case 2,
\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{E}^{\ell}
and
\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{E}^{\ell+1}
cocertify. Hence
\vec{C}^{\prime}
and, for
0\leq k\leq\ell+1
, all
\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{E}^{k}
cocertify, completing the inductive step.

Continue to iterate the inductive step, showing that
\vec{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}
and each
\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{E}^{k}
cocertify, until a
k
for which
\mathbb{C}_{k}
certifies is reached. We know that we will eventually reach such a
k
, since
\vec{\mathbb{C}}
certifies. At that point,
\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{E}^{k}
cocertifies with both
\vec{\mathbb{C}}
and
\vec{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}
, so
\vec{\mathbb{C}}
and
\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime}
cocertify, as required.
∎

Theorem 13.3
.

Suppose
\mathbb{C}
is a flagged chamber complex in
S^{3}
that is not tiny, and
S
is a balanced or almost balanced sphere transverse to
F(\mathbb{C})
. Let
E
be a disk disjoint from
S
properly embedded and essential in a chamber of
\mathbb{C}
that is not an empty torus. Let
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}
be the flagged chamber complex obtained from
\mathbb{C}
by decomposition along
E
. Suppose also that
S
is balanced or almost balanced for
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}
. Then any sequence in
\overrightarrow{(\mathbb{C},S)}
and any sequence in
\overrightarrow{(\mathbb{C}^{\prime},S)}
cocertify.

Note: the requirements that
E
be essential in its chamber and that
C
not be an empty torus will be removed later, see Corollary
15.11
.

Proof.

Let
F,F^{\prime}
denote the defining surfaces
F(\mathbb{C}),F(\mathbb{C}^{\prime})
respectively. The circles
F\cap S
and
F^{\prime}\cap S
determine decomposition sequences
\vec{\mathbb{C}}
and
\vec{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}
as described in Section
9
. Since
E
is essential and not the meridian of an empty torus, surgery on
E
creates no new goneballs, so in fact
F\cap S=F^{\prime}\cap S
. Thus the guiding disk sets
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
for the two decomposition sequences
\vec{\mathbb{C}}
and
\vec{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}
are the same. Import the notation for
\vec{\mathbb{C}}
and
\vec{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}
from the paragraph that precedes Proposition
13.2
to these decomposition sequences
\vec{\mathbb{C}}
and
\vec{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}
.

We are given that the chamber complexes
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}
, related by the decomposition
\mathbb{C}\xrightarrow{E}\mathbb{C}^{\prime}
, are each balanced or almost balanced.
As noted in the proof of Proposition
10.2
during the decomposition sequence the genus of the defining surface above
S
and below
S
does not change. Surgery on
E
may or may not lower by one the genus of the defining surface on the side of
S
in which
E
lies, but we are given that
S
is balanced or almost balanced for
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}
as well as for
\mathbb{C}
. It follows that for any
0\leq k\leq m
the chamber complex
\mathbb{C}^{k}_{k}
defined by the decomposition
\mathbb{C}_{k}\xrightarrow{E}\mathbb{C}^{k}_{k}
in
\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{E}^{k}
is also balanced or almost balanced.

Then Propositions
10.2
and
10.3
imply that the last term in each of the sequences
\vec{\mathbb{C}},\vec{\mathbb{C}}
and each
\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{E}^{k}
certifies. Then Proposition
13.2
then says that
\vec{\mathbb{C}}
and
\vec{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}
cocertify, as claimed.
∎

14.
Delaying a disk

In this section we consider what happens in a sequence of two flagged chamber complex decompositions if, when this is possible, a decomposition disk in the first decomposing disk set is transferred to the second decomposing disk set.

Suppose
\mathbb{C}_{0}
is a flagged chamber complex supporting
S^{3}=A\cup_{T}B
, and

\mathbb{C}_{0}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}_{0}}\mathbb{C}_{1}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}_{1}}\mathbb{C}_{2}

is a sequence of flagged chamber complex decompositions. Suppose
E
is a properly embedded disk in a chamber of
\mathbb{C}_{0}
and
E
is disjoint from both
\mathcal{D}_{0}
and
\mathcal{D}_{1}
. There are a variety of decomposition sequences that can be defined in this situation. Here is the notation we will use for them:

•

\mathbb{C}_{0}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}_{0}}\mathbb{C}_{1}\xrightarrow{E}\mathbb{C}_{0E}

•

\mathbb{C}_{1}\xrightarrow{E}\mathbb{C}_{E}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{1}}\mathbb{C}_{E1}

•

\mathbb{C}_{0}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}_{0}\cup E}\mathbb{C}_{0+E}

•

\mathbb{C}_{1}\xrightarrow{E\cup\mathcal{D}_{1}}\mathbb{C}_{E+1}

In the second of these sequences
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{1}
has the usual meaning: those disks in
\mathcal{D}_{1}
whose boundaries lie on the defining surface of
\mathbb{C}_{E}
(and so do not lie on the boundary of goneballs after decomposition by
E
). Note that if
\mbox{$\partial$}E
lies on the boundary of a goneball in
\hat{\mathbb{C}}_{1}
, then it does not lie on the defining surface for
\mathbb{C}_{1}
, so decomposing on a disk set containing
E
is the same as decomposing on the disk set without
E
. So in this situation
\mathbb{C}_{E1}=\mathbb{C}_{E+1}=\mathbb{C}_{2}
.

Lemma 14.1
.

Suppose neither
\mathbb{C}_{0}
nor
\mathbb{C}_{1}
certify. Then, up to a choice of siblings, if siblings exist, either

(1)

\mathbb{C}_{0E}=\mathbb{C}_{0+E}
and
\mathbb{C}_{E1}=\mathbb{C}_{E+1}
or

(2)

\mathbb{C}_{0E}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{C}_{0+E}
and
\mathbb{C}_{E1}=\mathbb{C}_{E+1}
or

(3)

\mathbb{C}_{0E}=\mathbb{C}_{0+E}
and
\mathbb{C}_{E1}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{C}_{E+1}
.

Proof.

Let
F_{0},F_{1}
be the defining surfaces for respectively
\mathbb{C}_{0},\mathbb{C}_{1}
. Suppose first that
\mbox{$\partial$}E
is separating in a component of
F_{0}-\mbox{$\partial$}\mathcal{D}_{0}
. Then it is separating in a component of
F_{1}
, because
F_{1}
is obtained from
F_{0}-\mbox{$\partial$}\mathcal{D}_{1}
by adding (scar) disks to
\mbox{$\partial$}\mathcal{D}_{1}
and perhaps deleting some spheres. Then
\mbox{$\partial$}E
(if it is not on the boundary of a goneball) is also separating in a component of
F_{1}-\mbox{$\partial$}D_{1}
. It follows from the last sentence of Proposition
12.12
that
\mathbb{C}_{E1}=\mathbb{C}_{E+1}
and, further applying Proposition
12.12
, that outcome (1) or (2) of Lemma
14.1
occurs.

On the other hand, if
\mbox{$\partial$}E
is non-separating in
F_{0}-\mbox{$\partial$}\mathcal{D}_{0}
then by Lemma
12.11

\mathbb{C}_{0E}=\mathbb{C}_{0+E}
. Then by Proposition
12.12
, outcome (1) or (3) occurs.
∎

We now put this in the context of decomposition sequences. Suppose
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{1},...,\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{n}
are a sequence of guiding disk sets in
S^{3}
so that
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{1}
determines the decomposition
\mathbb{C}_{1}\xrightarrow{E\cup\mathcal{D}_{1}}\mathbb{C}_{E+1}
above. Then the guiding disk sets
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
determine extensions of the three sequences that we label as follows:

\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{0+E}:\mathbb{C}_{0}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}_{0}\cup E}\mathbb{C}_{0+E}\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{1}}\mathbb{C}_{2}^{0+E}\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{2}}\mathbb{C}^{0+E}_{3}\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{3}}...\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{n-1}}\mathbb{C}^{0+E}_{n}

\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{E1}:\mathbb{C}_{0}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}_{0}}\mathbb{C}_{1}\xrightarrow{E}\mathbb{C}_{0E}\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{1}}\mathbb{C}_{2}^{0E}\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{2}}\mathbb{C}^{0E}_{3}\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{3}}...\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{n-1}}\mathbb{C}^{0E}_{n}

\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{E+1}:\mathbb{C}_{0}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}_{0}}\mathbb{C}_{1}\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{1}}\mathbb{C}_{E+1}\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{2}}\mathbb{C}^{E+1}_{3}\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{3}}...\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{n-1}}\mathbb{C}^{E+1}_{n}

Lemma 14.2
.

Suppose all three decomposition sequences certify. Then all three decomposition sequences cocertify.

Proof.

If
\mathbb{C}_{0}
certifies, this is obvious:
\mathbb{C}_{0}
is a term in all three sequences, so if it certifies then all three sequences cocertify.

If
\mathbb{C}_{1}
certifies, then
\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{E+1}
and
\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{E1}
cocertify for the same reason:
\mathbb{C}_{1}
is in each sequence. If further
\mathbb{C}_{0}
does not certify, we can apply Proposition
12.12
to conclude that either
\mathbb{C}_{0E}=\mathbb{C}_{0+E}
or
\mathbb{C}_{0E}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{C}_{0+E}
. In the former case the sequences

\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{E1}
and
\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{0+E}
also cocertify because, starting with
\mathbb{C}_{0E}=\mathbb{C}_{0+E}
, they are the same sequence and before that in
\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{0+E}
is only
\mathbb{C}_{0}
, which does not certify. In the latter case, when
\mathbb{C}_{0E}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{C}_{0+E}
, they cocertify by Corollary
11.12
.

Suppose neither
\mathbb{C}_{0}
nor
\mathbb{C}_{1}
certify. Then following Lemma
14.1
the other decompositions can be arranged towards the left in exactly one of the following three diagrams, which differ only in which of two possible deflations (shown as vertical dashed arrows) are in fact equalities:

\textstyle{\mathbb{C}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{D}_{0}\cup E}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{D}_{0}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}_{E+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}^{E+1}_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{3}}
\textstyle{...}
\textstyle{=\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{E+1}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}_{0E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}_{E1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{...}
\textstyle{=\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{E1}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}_{0+E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}^{0+E}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{2}}
\textstyle{...}
\textstyle{=\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{0+E}}

\textstyle{\mathbb{C}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{D}_{0}\cup E}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{D}_{0}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}_{E+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}^{E+1}_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{3}}
\textstyle{...}
\textstyle{=\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{E+1}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}_{0E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}_{E1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{...}
\textstyle{=\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{E1}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}_{0+E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}^{0+E}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{2}}
\textstyle{...}
\textstyle{=\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{0+E}}

\textstyle{\mathbb{C}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{D}_{0}\cup E}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{D}_{0}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}_{E+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}^{E+1}_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{3}}
\textstyle{...}
\textstyle{=\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{E+1}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}_{0E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}_{E1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{2}}
\textstyle{...}
\textstyle{=\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{E1}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}_{0+E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}^{0+E}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{2}}
\textstyle{...}
\textstyle{=\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{0+E}}

In the first diagram it is obvious that all three sequences cocertify: because of the equalities, all three sequences are in fact the same sequence after the terms
\mathbb{C}_{E+1},\mathbb{C}_{E1}
and
\mathbb{C}_{1}^{0+E}
. Similarly the sequences
\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{E+1}
and
\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{E1}
become the same sequence after those terms in the second diagram and so cocertify; the same is true (even one step earlier) for
\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{E1}
and
\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{0+E}
in the third diagram.

Finally, by Corollary
11.12
, both
\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{E1}
and
\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{0+E}
cocertify in the second diagram and
\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{E+1}
and
\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{E1}
cocertify in the third.
∎

15.
Ghost circles and timing of disks

Let
\mathbb{C}
be a flagged chamber complex in
S^{3}
supporting
A\cup_{T}B
, and
S\subset S^{3}
be a sphere transverse to
\mathbb{C}
. It was shown in Section
9
how
S
defines a flagged chamber complex decomposition sequence for
\mathbb{C}
. We briefly recall the process, filling in further description, assisted by augmented notation: For
F
the defining surface for
\mathbb{C}
, the innermost in
S
circles
\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}_{0}
of
F\cap S
bound disks in
S
which we regard as a disk set
\mathcal{D}_{0}
for
\mathbb{C}
. The set of circles
\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}_{1}
in
F\cap S
(denoted simply
\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}_{1}
in Section
9
)consists of those circles which bound disks in
S
whose interiors may contain circles in
\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}_{0}
but otherwise are disjoint from
F
. Call this set of disks
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{1}
. Proceeding in this manner partition the entire collection of circles
F\cap S
into sets
\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}_{0}=\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}_{0},\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}_{1}...,\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}_{n}
, where
n=\lfloor\frac{{\rm diam}(Y)+1}{2}\rfloor
, so that each circle in
\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}_{k}
bounds a disk
D
in
S
, and any circle of
F\cap S
that lies in the interior of
D
lies in some
\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}_{i},i<k
. Denote the collection of such disks in
S
bounded by the circles in
\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}_{k}
by
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{k}
.

The decomposition sequence

\vec{\mathbb{C}}:\quad\mathbb{C}=\mathbb{C}_{0}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}_{0}}\mathbb{C}_{1}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}_{1}}\mathbb{C}_{2}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}_{2}}...\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}_{n-1}}\mathbb{C}_{n}

is then iteratively defined, the first step being the decomposition
\mathbb{C}=\mathbb{C}_{0}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}_{0}}\mathbb{C}_{1}
. After this decomposition two important things happen to the sets of circles
\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}_{i},1\leq i\leq n
:

•

The circles
\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}_{0}
have been surgered away, so the interior of each disk in
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{1}
is disjoint from the defining surface
F_{1}
for
\mathbb{C}_{1}
.

•

Some of the circles in
\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}_{1}
may lie on the goneballs of the decomposition, and so no longer lie in
S\cap F_{1}
.

Then let
\mathcal{D}_{1}\subset\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{1}
be the subset of disks that are left, i. e. the subset of disks whose boundaries lie on
F_{1}
.

Since, after the decomposition by
\mathcal{D}_{0}
, the interiors of the disks
\mathcal{D}_{1}
are disjoint from
F_{1}
, they are a disk set in
\mathbb{C}_{1}
. Use these disks to define the decomposition
\mathbb{C}_{1}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}_{1}}\mathbb{C}_{2}
. Continue in this manner to get the entire decomposition sequence, noting that each
\mathcal{D}_{i}
in the construction consists of those disks in
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
whose boundaries lie on the defining surface
F_{i}=F(\mathbb{C}_{i})
, and not on the boundaries of goneballs from earlier decompositions in the sequence. In other words:

Lemma 15.1
.

For each
1\leq i\leq n-1
, the disk set
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
is a guiding disk set for the decomposition
\mathbb{C}_{i}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}_{i}}\mathbb{C}_{i+1}
. ∎

Definition 15.2
.

For each
1\leq i\leq n-1
, the circles
\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}_{i}-\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}_{i}
(that is
\mbox{$\partial$}\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{i}-\mbox{$\partial$}\mathcal{D}_{i}
) are called the ghost circles of such a decomposition sequence in
\overrightarrow{(\mathbb{C},S)}
. The circles
\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}_{i}=F_{i}\cap S
, that is the circles that bound the disks
\mathcal{D}_{i}\subset S
, will be called the
F
-circles of the decomposition sequence.

The disk set
\mathcal{D}_{i}
may well contain ghost circles in its interior; these indicate where
\mathcal{D}_{i}
intersects spheres bounding goneballs in earlier decompositions, and so can be ignored. Nevertheless, the ghost circles do play a role in the construction of the decomposition sequence, namely they are part of what determines, at the beginning of the decomposition sequence, before they are ghost circles, where in the sequence of decompositions a particular sub-disk of
S
appears.

Following up on this idea, it is reasonable to consider how introducing ghost circles in
S
at the beginning of the decomposition process (when the sets of circles
\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}_{i}
for the decomposition sequence are being defined) might affect the decomposition sequence. For example, if a ghost circle
c^{\prime}
were to be added, at the beginning of the decomposition sequence, parallel to a circle
c\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}_{i-1}
and just inside the disk
D\in\mathcal{D}_{i-1}
that
c
bounds, one effect would be to redefine
D
as a disk in
\mathcal{D}_{i}
, because
c
is no longer
i
-th innermost in
F\cap S
but
(i+1)
-st innermost in the collection of circles
\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}\cup c^{\prime}
. There could well be a similar delay to the use of other disks in the following
\mathcal{D}_{k},i<k<n
in a manner that may be a bit difficult to describe - see Figure
40
. Our next goal is to understand to some extent how adding or removing ghost circles (and thereby changing the location of the decomposing disks in the sequence of disk decompositions) affects the certification of a decomposition sequence. The news is good: eventually we will show that adding or removing ghost circles may alter the decomposition sequence, as we have seen, but the new decomposition sequence cocertifies with the old.

2pt

\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}_{2}
at 130 360

\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}_{3}

\endlabellist

Figure 40.
Adding a ghost circle,
i=3

To this end we generalize the discussion of Section
9
to spheres in
S
transverse to the defining surface
F
of a flagged chamber complex
\mathbb{C}
in
S^{3}
and consider a finite collection of circles
\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}
in
S
containing all of the circles
\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}=F\cap S
(which we have distinguished as
F
-circles
) but augmented by other possible circles
\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}-F
in
S
called ghost circles. The collection of circles
\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}
determines a sequence of flagged chamber complex decompositions guided by disk sets
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
, where each circle in
\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}
is the boundary of a disk in some
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
, as described above and in Section
9
. (This setting naturally arises in the middle of a flagged chamber complex decomposition sequence, as we have seen.)

As in Section
9
, let
Y
be the tree associated to the circles
\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}\subset S
, and, as in Definition
9.4
, let

\vec{\mathbb{C}}:\quad\mathbb{C}=\mathbb{C}_{0}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}_{0}}\mathbb{C}_{1}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}_{1}}\mathbb{C}_{2}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}_{2}}...\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}_{n-1}}\mathbb{C}_{n}

be the complete sequence of flagged chamber complex decompositions guided by the sphere
S
, where

n=\lfloor\frac{{\rm diam}(Y)+1}{2}\rfloor
. It’s perfectly possible that for some values of
i
,
\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}_{i}
consists entirely of ghost circles, so none of the disks
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{i}
have their boundaries on
F
and so
\mathcal{D}_{i}=\emptyset
. In this case the decomposition
\mathbb{C}_{i}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}_{i}}\mathbb{C}_{i+1}
is just the identity:
\mathbb{C}_{i}=\mathbb{C}_{i+1}
.

Recalling Definition
9.1
, each edge
e
in
Y
(corresponding to a circle
c\in\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}
) is assigned a value
0\leq\rho_{Y}(e)\leq\lfloor\frac{{\rm diam}(Y)-1}{2}\rfloor
. For each vertex
v\in Y
we can similarly assign
\rho_{Y}(v)=max\{\rho_{Y}(e)\;|\;e\text{ an edge incident to }v\}
.

Here are some elementary observations. For
e
an edge in
Y
, let
m^{e}_{\pm}
be the numbers defined in Definition
9.1
.

Lemma 15.3
.

There is an edge
f
in
Y
such that
m^{f}_{+}=m^{f}_{-}
if and only if
{\rm diam}(Y)
is odd. In this case
\rho_{Y}(f)=\frac{{\rm diam}(Y)-1}{2}
and
f
is unique: it is the mid-edge of each path in
Y
whose length is
{\rm diam}(Y)
. Call
f
the center edge of the tree
Y
. See Figure
40
.

Proof.

Let
e
be any edge in
Y
, with endpoints
v_{+}
and
v_{-}
belonging to the trees
Y_{+}
and
Y_{-}
respectively in
Y-e
, as discussed in Definition
9.1
. If there is a leaf
v_{\ell}
of
Y
that belongs to
Y_{+}
and is a distance
m>\frac{{\rm diam}(Y)-1}{2}
from
v_{+}
, then
v_{\ell}
is a distance
m+1>\frac{{\rm diam}(Y)+1}{2}
from
v_{-}
. It follows from the definition of diameter that no leaf of
Y
that belongs to
Y_{-}
can be a distance more than

{\rm diam}(Y)-m-1=\frac{{\rm diam}(Y)-1}{2}+\frac{{\rm diam}(Y)+1}{2}-m-1<\frac{{\rm diam}(Y)-1}{2}

from
v_{-}
. Hence
m_{-}\leq\frac{{\rm diam}(Y)-1}{2}<m\leq m_{+}
and
e
is not as
f
is described in the Lemma.

Thus an edge
f
as described in the Lemma has the property that any leaf of
Y
in
Y_{\pm}
has distance at most
\frac{{\rm diam}(Y)-1}{2}
from respectively the vertices
v_{\pm}
. This implies that any path in
Y_{\pm}
can have length at most
{\rm diam}(Y)-1
, so in particular no path
\gamma
in
Y
of length
{\rm diam}(Y)
can lie entirely in either of
Y_{\pm}
. This implies that
f
must be in
\gamma
and in fact be the mid-edge of
\gamma
, defining
f
uniquely and establishing that
\rho_{Y}(f)=m^{f}_{\pm}=\frac{{\rm diam}(Y)-1}{2}
.

Conversely, suppose
{\rm diam}(Y)
is odd and
f
is the mid-edge of a path
\gamma
in
Y
of length
{\rm diam}(Y)
. If any leaf in
Y
lying in
Y_{+}
(resp
Y_{-}
) is a distance greater than
\frac{{\rm diam}(Y)-1}{2}
from
v_{+}
(resp
v_{-}
) then conjoining a path from that leaf to
v_{+}
with the part of
\gamma-v_{+}
containing
v_{-}
would give a path of length greater than
{\rm diam}(Y)
, a contradiction. Hence both
m^{f}_{\pm}=\frac{{\rm diam}(Y)-1}{2}
as required.
∎

Quite similarly we have:

Lemma 15.4
.

There is a vertex
w
in
Y
that is incident to more than one edge
e
with
\rho_{Y}(e)=\rho_{Y}(w)
if and only if
{\rm diam}(Y)
is even. In this case
w
is unique and
\rho_{Y}(w)=\frac{{\rm diam}(Y)}{2}-1
. Call
w
the center vertex of the tree
Y
. See Figure
40
.

Proof.

Consider any vertex
v
in
Y
and suppose there is a path
\gamma
in
Y
from
v
to a leaf of
Y
and that the length of
\gamma
is
m>\frac{{\rm diam}(Y)}{2}
. Let
e
be the edge of
\gamma
that is adjacent to
v
and observe that, by definition of diameter, any path from
v
to a leaf of
Y
that is longer than
{\rm diam}(Y)-m<m
must pass through
e
. Definition
9.1
then implies that
\rho_{Y}(e)
is the length of the longest path in
Y

not passing through
e
from
v
to a leaf of
Y
, and any edge
e^{\prime}
incident to
v
other than
e
has
\rho_{Y}(e^{\prime})\leq\rho_{Y}(e)-1
. In particular,
e
is the only edge incident to
v
with
\rho_{Y}(e)=\rho_{Y}(v)
, so
e
is not an edge as described in the lemma.

It follows that if
w
is as described in the lemma, then the length of any path from
w
to a leaf in
Y
has length
\leq\frac{{\rm diam}(Y)}{2}
. So, by definition of diameter, any path in
Y
whose length is
{\rm diam}(Y)
must pass through
w
and have its length bisected by
w
. Thus
{\rm diam}(Y)
is even and the distance from
w
to a most distant leaf will be
\frac{{\rm diam}(Y)}{2}
. In particular, any edge
e
incident to
w
will find the vertex at its other end a distance of at most
\frac{{\rm diam}(Y)}{2}-1
from a leaf. Thus
\rho_{Y}(e)\leq\frac{{\rm diam}(Y)}{2}-1
. Since this is true for all edges incident to
w
,
\rho_{Y}(w)\leq\frac{{\rm diam}(Y)}{2}-1
. Furthermore, since any path in
Y
whose length is
{\rm diam}(Y)
must pass through
w
, there are at least two edges
e_{\pm}
incident to
w
lying on such a path, and then
\rho_{Y}(e_{\pm})=\frac{{\rm diam}(Y)}{2}-1
. It follows that for both edges
\rho_{Y}(e_{\pm})=\frac{{\rm diam}(Y)}{2}-1=\rho_{Y}(w)
as required.

Conversely, if
{\rm diam}(Y)
is even,
\gamma
is a path in
Y
of length
{\rm diam}(Y)
, and
w
is the vertex that bisects
\gamma
, the same argument shows that for
e
either of the two edges in
\gamma
incident to
w
,
\rho_{Y}(e)=\rho_{Y}(w)
.
∎

Lemma 15.5
.

Suppose
P\subset S-\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}
is the planar surface corresponding to a vertex
v\in Y
. If
P\subset D\in\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{k}
, then
\rho_{Y}(v)\leq k
.

Proof.

For
D\in\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{k}
,
\mbox{$\partial$}D\in\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}_{k}
and from Lemma
9.2
each circle in
\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}\cap\mbox{${\rm int}$}(D)
lies in some
\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}_{j},j<k
. Hence if
P\subset D
, each circle
c\in\mbox{$\partial$}P
lies in some
\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}_{j},j\leq k
. Corresponding to this, we have that each edge
e
in
Y
that is incident to
v
has
\rho_{Y}(e)\leq k
. Hence by definition
\rho_{Y}(v)\leq k
as required.
∎

Lemma 15.6
.

Suppose a ghost circle is added to
S
in a planar surface
P\subset S-\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}
corresponding to a vertex
v\in Y
and

\vec{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}:\quad\mathbb{C}=\mathbb{C}_{0}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{0}}\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{1}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{1}}\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{2}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{2}}...

is the resulting sequence of flagged chamber complex decompositions. Suppose
\rho_{Y}(v)\geq 1
. Then for each
1\leq i\leq\rho_{Y}(v)
,
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{i-1}=\mathcal{D}_{i-1}
and
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{i}=\mathbb{C}_{i}
.

Proof.

If
i\leq\rho_{Y}(v)
then
\rho_{Y}(v)\nleq i-1
. It follows from Lemma
15.5
that any disk
D\in\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{i-1}
does not contain
P
and so is unaffected by the addition of the ghost circle. Thus, for each
1\leq i\leq\rho_{Y}(v)
,
\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{i-1}=\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{i-1}
. Now argue iteratively: Since
C^{\prime}_{0}=C_{0}
and
\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{0}=\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
it follows that
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{0}=\mathcal{D}_{0}
. Hence
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{1}=\mathbb{C}_{1}
, so if
\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{1}=\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{1}
then
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{1}=\mathcal{D}_{1}
. The argument continues iteratively until
i=\rho_{Y}(v)
: For each
1\leq i\leq\rho_{Y}(v)
,
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{i-1}=\mathbb{C}_{i-1}
and
\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{i-1}=\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{i-1}
imply
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{i-1}=\mathcal{D}_{i-1}
, so
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{i}=\mathbb{C}_{i}
, as required.
∎

Corollary 15.7
.

Suppose
{\rm diam}(Y)
is even and a ghost circle is added to
S
in the planar surface
P\subset S-\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}
corresponding to the center vertex
v\in Y
. Then
\vec{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}=\vec{\mathbb{C}}
.

Proof.

Since
{\rm diam}(Y)
is even, the complete sequence
\vec{\mathbb{C}}
has last term
\mathbb{C}_{n}
with
n={\rm diam}(Y)/2=\rho_{Y}(v)
. If
\rho_{Y}(v)=0
then
Y
is a star graph, each circle in
\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}
bounds a unique disk in
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
and both
\vec{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}
and
\vec{\mathbb{C}}
are simply the sequence
\mathbb{C}_{0}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}_{0}}\mathbb{C}_{1}
. Indeed, after this decomposition the only circle remaining in
S
is the added ghost circle, so
S\cap F(\mathbb{C}_{1})=\emptyset
and there is no further decomposition in the sequence. If, on the other hand,
\rho_{Y}(v)\geq 1
then by Lemma
15.6
, for all
0\leq i\leq n
,
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{i}=\mathbb{C}_{i}
and after these decompositions, the only circle that might remain is again the added ghost circle.
∎

Corollary 15.8
.

Suppose
{\rm diam}(Y)
is odd and a ghost circle is added to
S
in a planar surface
P\subset S-\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}
corresponding to a vertex at the end of the center edge
e\in Y
. Then if
\vec{\mathbb{C}}
certifies,
\vec{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}
and
\vec{\mathbb{C}}
cocertify.

Proof.

One possibility is that
{\rm diam}(Y)=1
, that is
Y
is the single edge
e
. In this case
\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}
is a single circle
c
(corresponding to
e
) dividing
S
into two disks
D_{A}
and
D_{B}
, as in Lemma
10.4
. Say the ghost circle is added to
D_{A}=P
. If
c
is also a ghost circle, then
F
is disjoint from
S
and there is no further decomposition. In particular,
\vec{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}=\vec{\mathbb{C}}
. If, on the other hand,
c
is an
F
-circle, so
c=F\cap S
then it is shown in Lemma
10.4
that if
\vec{\mathbb{C}}
certifies, the choice of whether to decompose
\mathbb{C}
along
D_{A}
or
D_{B}
makes no difference: the two results cocertify. Adding a ghost circle to
D_{A}
has the sole effect of declaring that in
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}
the decomposition will be along
D_{B}
(since
D_{A}
is no longer a disk component of
S-\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}
). Hence
\vec{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}
and
\vec{\mathbb{C}}
cocertify.

If, on the other hand,
{\rm diam}(Y)\geq 3
then, per Lemma
15.3

\rho_{Y}(v)=\rho_{Y}(e)=\frac{{\rm diam}(Y)-1}{2}\geq 1
. Then according to Lemma
15.6
the decomposition sequences
\vec{\mathbb{C}}
and
\vec{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}
are identical through
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{\frac{{\rm diam}(Y)-1}{2}}=\mathbb{C}_{\frac{{\rm diam}(Y)-1}{2}}
. But for
\mathbb{C}_{\frac{{\rm diam}(Y)-1}{2}}
,
\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}
has shrunk to a single circle, and
Y
is a single edge. Thus we can apply the argument just given for this case and conclude that
\vec{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}\sim\vec{\mathbb{C}}
as required.
∎

Proposition 15.9
.

Suppose
\mathbb{C}
is a flagged chamber complex in
S^{3}
that is not tiny. Let
S\subset S^{3}
be a sphere, transverse to the defining surface
F=F(\mathbb{C})
, that is either balanced or almost balanced. Let
\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}
be a collection of circles in
S
containing the set
\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}=F\cap S
and let
\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}^{\prime}
be the union of
\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}
and a disjoint (ghost) circle
c^{\prime}
in
S
. Let
\vec{\mathbb{C}}
and
\vec{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}
be the sequences of flagged chamber complex decompositions determined by
\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}
and
\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}^{\prime}
respectively. Then
\vec{\mathbb{C}}
and
\vec{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}
cocertify.

Proof.

Let
Y
be the tree associated to
\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}
,
P\subset S-\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}
be the planar surface component to which the ghost circle
c^{\prime}
is added, and
v
be the vertex in
Y
corresponding to
P
. The proof is by induction on the distance between
v
and the center vertex or edge of
Y
. Corollaries
15.7
and
15.8
show that the proposition is true when that distance is
0
.

Following Lemma
15.3
, as used in the proof of Corollary
15.7
, the decomposition sequences are identical until the tree has been shrunk to the point that
v
is a leaf. So we may as well assume that
v
is a leaf of
Y
, so
P
is an innermost disk of
S-\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}
. Let
\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}^{\prime\prime}
be the collection of circles
\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}\cup c^{\prime\prime}
, where
c^{\prime\prime}
is a circle parallel to
\mbox{$\partial$}P
but just
outside

P
, that is in the component
P^{\prime\prime}
of
S-\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}
that is adjacent to
P
. See Figure
41
. Then the vertex
v^{\prime\prime}
corresponding to
P^{\prime\prime}
is closer to the center edge or vertex of
Y
so, by inductive assumption, the sequence of flagged chamber complex decompositions
\vec{\mathbb{C}^{\prime\prime}}
determined by
\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}^{\prime\prime}
and
\vec{\mathbb{C}}
cocertify. So all that remains is to show that
\vec{\mathbb{C}^{\prime\prime}}
and
\vec{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}
cocertify.

\endlabellist

Figure 41.
Moving a ghost circle, so
\mbox{$\partial$}P\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}^{\prime}_{3}\to\mbox{$\partial$}P\in\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}^{\prime\prime}_{2}

If
\mbox{$\partial$}P
is a ghost circle, then
\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}^{\prime\prime}=\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}^{\prime}
so in fact
\vec{\mathbb{C}^{\prime\prime}}=\vec{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}
and we are done. So assume that
\mbox{$\partial$}P
is an
F
-circle in
\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}
and note that the only difference between
\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}^{\prime\prime}
and
\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}^{\prime}
is the side on which a ghost circle is added. This switch in sides has no effect on the tree and so no effect on the disks in the decomposition sequence
\vec{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}
except this: In
\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime\prime}
the disk
P
is in
\mathcal{D}_{0}
, since
P
is an innermost disk, whereas in
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}
the disk
P
is in
\mathcal{D}_{1}
because the disk bounded by
\mbox{$\partial$}P
contains the single added ghost circle
c^{\prime}
.

Apply Lemma
14.2
to this situation, where here the disk
P
plays the role of
E
in that lemma,
\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime\prime}
here corresponds to
\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{0+E}
there and
\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime}
here corresponds to
\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{E+1}
there. Since
S
is balanced or almost balanced, both sequences
\vec{\mathbb{C}^{\prime\prime}}
and
\vec{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}
certify, as does the sequence
\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{P1}
, defined for
P
as
\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{E1}
is defined for
E
in the preamble to Lemma
14.2
. The conclusion of Lemma
14.2
then says that
\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime\prime}
and
\vec{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}
cocertify, so
\vec{\mathbb{C}}\sim\vec{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime\prime}\sim\vec{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}
, as required.
∎

Corollary 15.10
.

Suppose
\mathbb{C}
is a flagged chamber complex in
S^{3}
that is not tiny and
S\subset S^{3}
is a sphere transverse to
F=F(\mathbb{C})
which is either balanced or almost balanced for
\mathbb{C}
. Let
\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}
and
\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}^{\prime}
be collections of circles in
S
, each containing the set
\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}=F\cap S
. Let
\vec{\mathbb{C}}
and
\vec{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}
be the sequences of flagged chamber complex decompositions determined by
\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}
and
\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}^{\prime}
respectively. Then
\vec{\mathbb{C}}
and
\vec{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}
cocertify.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the Corollary in the case that
\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}=\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}
. In this case
\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}^{\prime}
is obtained from
\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}
by adding some number
m
of ghost circles. But this case follows by applying Proposition
15.9

m
times.
∎

Corollary 15.11
.

Theorem
13.3
remains true even when the chamber
C
containing
E
is an empty torus, or when
E
is inessential.

Proof.

Referring to the proof of Theorem
13.3
, the requirement that
E
be essential and
C
not be an empty torus is to ensure that the original decomposition
C=C_{0}\xrightarrow{E}C^{\prime}
creates no goneballs. The boundaries of such goneballs would intersect
F=F(\mathbb{C})
in a family of circles
\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}_{G}
in
F\cap S
that continue to determine which circles in
F\cap S
bound disks in each
\mathcal{D}_{i}
during the subsequent decomposition

\vec{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}:\quad\mathbb{C}^{\prime}=\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{0}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{0}}\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{1}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{1}}\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{2}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{2}}...\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{n-1}}\mathbb{C}^{\prime}_{n}

used in the proof of Theorem
13.3
.

In contrast, it is the collection of circles
F(\mathbb{C}^{\prime})\cap S
that determines a decomposition sequence in
\overrightarrow{(\mathbb{C}^{\prime},S)}
and the goneballs of the decomposition
C=C_{0}\xrightarrow{E}C^{\prime}
do not appear in the definition of
F^{\prime}=F(\mathbb{C}^{\prime})
. In other words,
F\cap S=(F^{\prime}\cap S)\cup\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}_{G}
, so
\mbox{$\overline{\mbox{$\mathfrak{c}$}}$}_{G}
acts as a collection of ghost circles in the decomposition sequence
\vec{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}
. Corollary
15.10
says that the addition of such ghost circles makes no difference in certification. That is, we may as well assume that
F\cap S=F^{\prime}\cap S
and proceed with the proof of Theorem
13.3
as written.
∎

16.
Isotopies of guiding spheres

Definition 16.1
.

Suppose
\mathbb{C}
is a flagged chamber complex in
S^{3}
and
S
,
S^{\prime}
are spheres transverse to the defining surface
F=F(\mathbb{C})
. If any
\vec{\mathbb{C}}\in\overrightarrow{(\mathbb{C},S)}
and
\vec{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}\in\overrightarrow{(\mathbb{C},S^{\prime})}
cocertify, then we say
\overrightarrow{(\mathbb{C},S)}
and
\overrightarrow{(\mathbb{C},S^{\prime})}
cocertify and write
\overrightarrow{(\mathbb{C},S)}\sim\overrightarrow{(\mathbb{C},S^{\prime})}
.

Our goal in this section is to prove:

Theorem 16.2
.

Suppose
\mathbb{C}
is a flagged chamber complex in
S^{3}
that is not tiny and supports the Heegaard splitting
S^{3}=A\cup_{T}B
. Suppose further that
S_{0}
and
S_{1}
are balanced or almost balanced spheres for
\mathbb{C}
that are isotopic in
S^{3}
through balanced or almost balanced spheres
S_{t},0\leq t\leq 1
. Then
\overrightarrow{(\mathbb{C},S_{0})}\sim\overrightarrow{(\mathbb{C},S_{1})}
.

As usual, let
F
denote the defining surface
F=F(\mathbb{C})
and put the isotopy in general position so that generically
S_{t}
is transverse to
F
but for certain discrete values of
t
,
S_{t}
is tangent to
F
at a single point, a non-degenerate critical point of index zero (
F
has a local max at
v
in a collar of
S
), two (a local min) or one (a saddle tangency).

Lemma 16.3
.

Theorem
16.2
is true if the isotopy
S_{t}
passes through only a single critical point, of index zero or two (a max or a min).

Proof.

The cases of index zero or index two are symmetric. Let
S_{m(iddle)}
be the sphere on which the critical point
v
occurs, and, in a collar of
S_{m}
, we may suppose
F
has a minimum. Call the bottom of the collar
S_{b(elow)}
and the top
S_{a(bove)}
. We can assume that other than the local minimum,
F
intersects the collar in vertical cylinders. Then
F\cap S_{a}
is the union of
F\cap S_{b}
and a single circle
c
, where
c
bounds a disk
D_{F}
in
F-S_{a}
(the disk in
F
containing
v
) and a parallel disk
D_{S}
in
S_{a}-F
. Let
\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{b}
(resp
\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{a}
) be the sequence of flagged chamber complex decompositions determined by
\mathbb{C}
and
S_{b}
(resp.
S_{a}
). Thus
\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{b}\in\overrightarrow{(\mathbb{C},S_{1})}
(say) and
\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{a}\in\overrightarrow{(\mathbb{C},S_{0})}
. See Figure
42
.
Augment the collection of circles
F\cap S_{b}
with the single (ghost) circle
c^{\prime}
parallel in
S_{b}
to
c\subset S_{a}
and call the resulting sphere with ghost circle
S_{c}
. Let
\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{c}
be a sequence of flagged chamber complex decompositions determined by
\mathbb{C}
and
S_{c}
. Then by Corollary
15.10

\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{b}\sim\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{c}
.

Passing

through a minimum

Now compare
\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{a}
with
\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{c}
. The pattern of circles in
F\cap S_{a}
is the same as in
F\cap S_{c}
except that the
F
-circle
c
in
S_{a}
appears as a ghost circle
c^{\prime}
in
S_{c}
. Thus the decomposition
\mathbb{C}_{0}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}_{0}}\mathbb{C}_{1}
is the same in each sequence, except in
\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{c}
the ghost circle
c^{\prime}
is just removed, whereas in
\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{a}
,
F
is surgered by
D_{F}
, creating an extra ball chamber
B_{G}
bounded by the sphere
D_{S}\cup D_{F}
.
But our convention for flagging declares the handlebody
B_{G}
to be empty and therefore a goneball, so the result of the flagged chamber complex decomposition
\mathbb{C}_{1}
is the same in both cases, as is the remaining pattern of circles
S_{a}\cap F(\mathbb{C}_{1})=S_{c}\cap F(\mathbb{C}_{1})
. It follows that the entire ensuing flagged chamber complex decomposition sequence is the same, so
\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{a}\sim\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{c}
. Combining, we have
\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{a}\sim\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{c}\sim\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{b}
so
\overrightarrow{(\mathbb{C},S_{0})}\sim\overrightarrow{(\mathbb{C},S_{1})}
as required.
∎

Next suppose the isotopy
S_{t}
has a single critical point that is a saddle tangency with
F
at a point
v\in F
.
As in the proof of Lemma
16.3
we consider how
F
intersects a collar of
S_{m}
, the sphere of the tangency at
v
, as shown in Figure
43
. A neighborhood in
F
of
S_{m}\cap F
(a figure eight) is a pair of pants with three disjoint circles:
c_{b}
, whose parallel in
S_{m}
is incident to
v
in two points, and
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
each of whose parallels in
S_{m}
is incident to
v
in a single point. With
S_{a}
and
S_{b}
as in Lemma
16.3
, we can assume that
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
both lie in
S_{a}
, and
c
lies in
S_{b}
.

As in Section

let
Y_{a}

) be the tree defined by the collection of circles

(resp
F\cap S_{b}
),
e_{1},e_{2}

Y_{a}
corresponding to
c_{1},c_{2}
and

be the edge in
Y_{b}
corresponding to
c_{b}
. As defined in
9.1
, let
\rho_{i}=\rho_{Y_{a}}(e_{i}),i=1,2
and
\rho_{b}=\rho_{Y_{b}}(e_{b})
. Put another way, in the decomposition sequences of
\mathbb{C}
guided by
S_{a}
and
S_{b}
, each of the two circles
c_{i},i=1,2
bounds a disk
D_{i}\in\mathcal{D}_{\rho_{i}}
in the decomposition
\mathbb{C}_{\rho_{i}}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}_{\rho_{i}}}\mathbb{C}_{\rho_{i}+1}
and
c_{b}
bounds a disk
D_{b}\in\mathcal{D}_{\rho_{b}}
in the decomposition
\mathbb{C}_{\rho_{b}}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}_{\rho_{b}}}\mathbb{C}_{\rho_{b}+1}
. With no loss of generality, assume
\rho_{1}\leq\rho_{2}
(i. e.
\mathcal{D}_{\rho_{1}}
does not appear after
\mathcal{D}_{\rho_{2}}
in the decomposition sequence). There are two possibilities for the disks
D_{1},D_{2}\subset S_{a}
, and the two viewpoints in Figure
43
are chosen so that in either case each
D_{i}
is on the bounded side of
c_{i}
in
S_{a}
: either
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
are disjoint, as shown in the left side of the figure, or
D_{1}\subset D_{2}
as shown in the right side. That is, the two viewpoints in the figure are chosen so that in each the
D_{i}
appear as bounded regions. Similarly,
D_{b}
can either lie on the side of
c_{b}
that contains
v
(the bounded side in the left of the figure and unbounded side in the right) or the other side. We have:

Lemma 16.4
.

If
D_{b}
lies on the side of
c_{b}
that contains
v
, then
\rho_{1}\leq\rho_{b}
.

Proof.

Recall that, aside from the component that contains
v
,
F
intersects the collar of
S
only in vertical annuli. Hence the interior of the bounded side of
c_{b}
on the left of Figure
43
(or the the interior of the unbounded side of
c_{b}
on the right) contains a parallel copy of each circle of
F\cap\mbox{${\rm int}$}(D_{1})
.
∎

Lemma 16.5
.

Theorem
16.2
is true if the isotopy
S_{t}
passes through only a single critical point, of index one (a saddle tangency).

Proof.

We make two claims:

Claim 1:
With no loss of generality, we can assume that
\rho_{1}\leq\rho_{b}
.

Proof of Claim 1:
By Lemma
16.4
this is true when
D_{b}
lies on the side of
c_{b}
that contains
v
, so suppose
D_{b}
lies on the other side. That is, suppose
D_{b}
lies on the outside (unbounded side) of
c_{b}
in the left of Figure
43
or on the inside (bounded side) of
c_{b}
on the right. Suppose
\rho_{b}<\rho_{1}
and consider what happens if we add
\rho_{1}-\rho_{b}
ghost circles to
D_{b}
, parallel to
\mbox{$\partial$}D_{b}
. This raises
\rho_{b}
so that
\rho_{b}=\rho_{1}
and, since there is a copy of the pattern of circles
F\cap D_{1}
on the other side of
c_{b}
in
S_{b}
, that is in
S_{b}-D_{b}
, (see left panel in Figure
44
) we do not change the side of
c_{b}
on which
D_{b}
lies. Let
S_{b}^{+}
denote
S_{b}
with these ghost circles added, and denote by
\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{b}^{+}
be the sequence of flagged chamber complex decompositions on
\mathbb{C}
determined by
S_{b}^{+}
. By Corollary
15.10
it suffices to show that
\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{b}^{+}
and
\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{a}
cocertify. This proves Claim 1.

\pinlabel

at 105 15

\pinlabel
F^{\prime}
at 450 50

\pinlabel
S_{a}
at 180 140

\pinlabel
S_{b}
at 200 30

\endlabellist

Figure 44.
When
D_{b}
lies on the side of
c_{b}
not containing
v

Since, per the claim,
\rho_{1}\leq\rho_{b}
and by definition
\rho_{1}\leq\rho_{2}
there is no difference between the decomposition sequences for
\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{a}
and
\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{b}
until we reach the decomposition
\mathbb{C}_{\rho_{1}}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{D}_{\rho_{1}}}\mathbb{C}_{\rho_{1}+1}
.
F_{\rho_{1}}=F(\mathbb{C}_{\rho_{1}})
still appears as in Figure
43
, but with the added information that
\mbox{${\rm int}$}(D_{1})
contains neither ghost circles nor
F
-circles. In particular, it is possible to define a new chamber complex
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}
obtained from
\mathbb{C}_{\rho_{1}}
by decomposing along
D_{1}
alone. That is

\mathbb{C}_{\rho_{1}}\xrightarrow{D_{1}}\mathbb{C}^{\prime}.

Claim 2:
Both
S_{a}
and
S_{b}
are still balanced or almost balanced for
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}
.

Proof of Claim 2:
Let
F^{\prime}
be the defining surface
F(\mathbb{C}^{\prime})
. See Figure
44
. We name 4 regions and 6 genera:

•

X
is the region below
S_{b}
and
g_{X}
is the genus of
F_{\rho_{1}}\cap X=F^{\prime}\cap X
.

•

X^{+}\supset X
is the region below
S_{a}
,
g_{X}^{+}
is the genus of
F_{\rho_{1}}\cap X^{+}
, and
g^{\prime+}_{X}
is the genus of
F^{\prime}\cap X^{+}
.

•

Y
is the region above
S_{a}
,
g_{Y}
is the genus of
F_{\rho_{1}}\cap Y
, and
g^{\prime}_{Y}
is the genus of
F^{\prime}\cap Y

•

Y^{+}\supset Y
is the region above
S_{b}
, and
g^{\prime+}_{Y}
is the genus of
F^{\prime}\cap Y^{+}

Surgery on
D_{1}\subset S_{a}
will not affect the genus of the part of
F_{\rho_{1}}
lying below or above
S_{a}
, so

(0)
g^{\prime+}_{X}=g^{+}_{X}
,
g^{\prime}_{Y}=g_{Y}
and
S_{a}
is balanced or almost balanced for
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}
.

To prove the claim, we then need only focus on
S_{b}
. Here are two further observations:

(1)

Since
F_{\rho_{1}}\cap X^{+}
is obtained from
F_{\rho_{1}}\cap X
by attaching a (genus 0) pair of pants on a single boundary component,
g^{+}_{X}=g_{X}
.

(2)

Since
F^{\prime}\cap Y^{+}
is obtained from
F^{\prime}\cap Y
by attaching a (genus 0) annulus on a single boundary component,
g^{\prime+}_{Y}=g^{\prime}_{Y}
.

By definition
S_{b}
is balanced or almost balanced for
\mathbb{C}^{\prime}
if and only if the pair of numbers
\{g_{X},g^{\prime+}_{Y}\}\neq\{0,k\},k\geq 2
. Indeed, by definition, the pair is
\{0,0\}
if and only if
S_{b}
is planar balanced, it is
\{0,1\}
if and only if
S_{b}
is almost balanced, and the pair is
\{j,k\},j,k\geq 1
if and only if
S_{b}
is non-planar balanced. Similarly, since
S_{a}
is balanced or almost balanced for
\mathbb{C}_{\rho_{1}}
, we have
\{g^{+}_{X},g_{Y}\}\neq\{0,k\},k\geq 2
. From (1) we have
g_{X}=g^{+}_{X}
and combining (0) and (2) gives
g^{\prime+}_{Y}=g_{Y}
. In particular,
\{g_{X},g^{\prime+}_{Y}\}=\{g^{+}_{X},g_{Y}\}\neq\{0,k\},k\geq 2
, completing the proof of Claim 2.

), so the decomposition sequence on

\vec{\mathbb{C}^{\prime}}
. As in the proof of Lemma

\vec{\mathbb{C}}_{b}

(say) and

. Now apply, for both
S_{a}

13.3
as augmented by Corollary
15.11
to conclude
